, and
(x,y)\in L
with probability
\{q+r(1-p-q)\}
.

(v)

When
(x+1,y)\in L
and
(x,y+1)\in D
(an analogous situation arises when
(x+1,y)\in D
and
(x,y+1)\in L
),
P_{1}
wins if
(x,y)
has been labeled as a trap, or if both the vertex
(x,y)
and the edge
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
are open. The game results in a draw if
(x,y)
is open, the edge
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
has been labeled as a trap and the edge
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
is open, while in all other cases,
P_{2}
wins. Therefore, we have
(x,y)\in W
with probability
\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}
,
(x,y)\in D
with probability
(1-p-q)r(1-r)
, and
(x,y)\in L
with probability
\{q+r^{2}(1-p-q)\}
.

(vi)

Finally, when both vertices of
\Out(x,y)
are in
D
,
P_{1}
wins if
(x,y)
is a trap, the game results in a draw if
(x,y)
is open and at least one of the edges
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
and
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
is open, and in all other cases,
P_{2}
wins. Therefore,
(x,y)\in W
with probability
p
,
(x,y)\in D
with probability
(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})
and
(x,y)\in L
with probability
\{q+r^{2}(1-p-q)\}
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we identify
S_{k}
with a copy of the integer line
\mathbb{Z}
, by identifying the vertex
(x,k-x)
on
S_{k}
with
x
on
\mathbb{Z}
. For an arbitrary but fixed
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\omega(x)\in\hat{\mathcal{A}}=\{W,L,D\}
, referred to as the
state
of the vertex
(x,k+1-x)
, denote the subset (out of
W
,
L
and
D
) to which
(x,k+1-x)
belongs, for each
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. Conditioned on the infinite tuple
\omega=(\omega(x):x\in\mathbb{Z})
that specifies the state of
each
vertex on
S_{k+1}
, the
random
state of the vertex
(x,k-x)
, lying on
S_{k}
, equals
\G_{p,q,r}\omega(x)
for each
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, as is evident from the recurrence relations described above, and from (
3.1
), (
3.2
), (
3.3
), (
3.4
), (
3.5
), (
3.6
) and (
3.7
)). This is how the game rules governing the generalized percolation game give rise to the PCA
\G_{p,q,r}
.

6.2.
Game rules for the bond percolation game

The recurrence relations arising from the bond percolation game described in §
2
can be deduced in much the same manner as those derived in §
6.1
for the generalized percolation game. We let an arbitrarily chosen
(x,y)\in S_{k}
, for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, be the initial vertex, and assume, as before, that
P_{1}
plays the first round of the game while
P_{2}
plays the second. We then deduce the state of the vertex
(x,y)
, i.e. which of the subsets
W
,
L
and
D
it belongs to, as follows:

(i)

When both vertices of
\Out(x,y)
are in
W
,
P_{1}
loses unless at least one of the edges
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
and
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
has been labeled as a target, so that
(x,y)\in L
with probability
(1-s^{\prime})^{2}
and
(x,y)\in W
with probability
(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})
.

(ii)

When
(x+1,y)\in L
and
(x,y+1)\in W
(analogously, when
(x+1,y)\in W
and
(x,y+1)\in L
),
P_{1}
wins unless the edge
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
has been labeled as a trap and the edge
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
is not a target. Hence,
(x,y)\in L
with probability
r^{\prime}(1-s^{\prime})
, and
(x,y)\in W
with probability
\{1-r^{\prime}(1-s^{\prime})\}
.

(iii)

When both vertices of
\Out(x,y)
are in
L
,
P_{1}
wins unless both the edges
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
and
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
are traps. Therefore,
(x,y)\in L
with probability
{r^{\prime}}^{2}
and
(x,y)\in W
with probability
(1-{r^{\prime}}^{2})
.

(iv)

When
(x+1,y)\in W
and
(x,y+1)\in D
(analogously, when
(x+1,y)\in D
and
(x,y+1)\in W
),
P_{1}
wins if at least one of the edges
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
and
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
has been labeled as a target, the game results in a draw if
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
is not a target and
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
is open, and in every other case,
P_{2}
wins. Thus,
(x,y)\in W
with probability
\{1-(1-s^{\prime})^{2}\}
,
(x,y)\in D
with probability
(1-s^{\prime})(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})
, and
(x,y)\in L
with probability
r^{\prime}(1-s^{\prime})
.

(v)

When
(x+1,y)\in L
and
(x,y+1)\in D
(analogously, when
(x+1,y)\in D
and
(x,y+1)\in L
),
P_{1}
wins as long as the edge
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
is not a trap or at least one of the edges
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
and
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
is a target, the game results in a draw if
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
is a trap and
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
is open, and in all other cases,
P_{2}
wins. Thus,
(x,y)\in W
with probability
\{1-r^{\prime}(1-s^{\prime})\}
,
(x,y)\in D
with probability
r^{\prime}(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})
, and
(x,y)\in L
with probability
{r^{\prime}}^{2}
.

(vi)

When both vertices of
\Out(x,y)
are in
D
,
P_{1}
wins if at least one of
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
and
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
is a target, the game results in a draw if neither
\big((x,y),(x+1,y)\big)
nor
\big((x,y),(x,y+1)\big)
is a target but not both are traps, and
P_{2}
wins otherwise. Thus,
(x,y)\in W
with probability
\{1-(1-s^{\prime})^{2}\}
,
(x,y)\in D
with probability
(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})(1+r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})
, and
(x,y)\in L
with probability
{r^{\prime}}^{2}
.

As argued in §
6.1
, the above-mentioned recurrence relations or game rules governing the bond percolation game are captured precisely by the stochastic update rules of the PCA
\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
in (
3.8
), (
3.9
), (
3.10
), (
3.11
), (
3.12
) and (
3.13
).

7.
Proofs of Theorems
1
,
2
and
3
, assuming Theorems
4
and
5
to be true

Let us consider a generalized percolation game on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. For each fixed realization of the random assignment of labels (as described in §
2
) to the vertices and edges of our graph
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, each vertex
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
can be classified uniquely into one of the subsets
W
,
D
and
L
(dictated by the outcome of the game whose initial vertex is
(x,y)
), and we refer to this subset as the
state
of
(x,y)
. Let
\boldsymbol{\eta}(x,y)
denote the
random
state of
(x,y)
induced by the random assignment of labels to the vertices and edges of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Since
all
vertices of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
bear labels that are i.i.d., and
all
edges of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
bear labels that are i.i.d. as well, the joint law
\mu_{k}
of the random infinite tuple
\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}(x,y):(x,y)\in S_{k}\right)=\left(\boldsymbol{\eta}(x,k-x):x\in\mathbb{Z}\right)
is the same for
every

k\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that we may denote this common law henceforth by
\mu
(with no dependence on
k
). Referring to the game rules explained in §
6.1
and how they can be represented by
\G_{p,q,r}
defined in §
3
, we can write
\G_{p,q,r}\mu_{k+1}=\mu_{k}
, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Combining the two observations made above, we conclude that
\G_{p,q,r}\mu=\mu
, thus proving that
\mu
is a stationary distribution for
\G_{p,q,r}
. Moreover, the i.i.d. nature of the assignment of labels to the vertices and edges of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
ensures that
\mu
is both
translation-invariant
and
reflection-invariant
. By these two attributes, we mean the following:

(i)

Recall, from the last paragraph of §
6.1
, that we identify
S_{k}
with
\mathbb{Z}
by mapping
(x,k-x)
onto
x
for each
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. Consequently,
\mu
can be thought of as a probability measure on
\Omega=\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathbb{Z}}=\{W,L,D\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. For any
y\in\mathbb{Z}
, we let
\mathfrak{T}^{y}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega
map any configuration
\eta=(\eta(x):x\in\mathbb{Z})
to the configuration
\mathfrak{T}^{y}\eta=\left(T^{y}\eta(x):x\in\mathbb{Z}\right)
where
\mathfrak{T}^{y}\eta(x)=\eta(x+y)
for each
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. A probability measure
\nu
, defined with respect to the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}
generated by all the cylinder sets of
\Omega
, is said to be
translation-invariant
if
\nu(B)=\nu\left(\mathfrak{T}^{y}B\right)
for every
B\in\mathbb{D}
, where
\mathfrak{T}^{y}B=\left\{\mathfrak{T}^{y}\eta:\eta\in B\right\}
.

(ii)

Let
\mathfrak{R}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega
map any configuration
\eta=(\eta(x):x\in\mathbb{Z})
to the configuration
\mathfrak{R}\eta=\left(\mathfrak{R}\eta(x):x\in\mathbb{Z}\right)
where
\mathfrak{R}\eta(x)=\eta(-x)
for each
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. A probability measure
\nu
, defined with respect to
\mathcal{F}
, is said to be
reflection-invariant
if
\nu(B)=\nu\left(\mathfrak{R}B\right)
for every
B\in\mathbb{D}
, where
\mathfrak{R}B=\left\{\mathfrak{R}\eta:\eta\in B\right\}
.

We now state what can be considered the most technically challenging result of our paper (the measure
\mu
in the statement of Theorem
4
need not be the same as the one alluded to in the previous paragraph):

Theorem 4
.

Let
(p,q,r)\in\Theta
satisfy the constraints stated in Theorem
1
, i.e. each of
p
,
q
and
r
is sufficiently small, the inequality in (
2.1
) holds, and precisely one of (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) is true. If
\mu
is any translation-invariant and reflection-invariant stationary distribution for
\G_{p,q,r}
, then for any fixed but arbitrary
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, the probability, under
\mu
, that
x
is assigned the state
D
, abbreviated as
\mu(D)
, equals
0
.

An analogous result is true in case of bond percolation games, and can be stated as follows:

Theorem 5
.

Let
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\Theta^{\prime}
belong to any one of the three regimes described in Theorem
2
, i.e. one of
(B1)
,
(B2)
and
(B3)
. If
\mu
is any translation-invariant and reflection-invariant stationary distribution for
\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
, then for any fixed but arbitrary
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, the probability, under
\mu
, that
x
is assigned the state
D
, abbreviated simply as
\mu(D)
, equals
0
.

The proofs of Theorems
4
and
5
are accomplished in §
8
and §
9
respectively, employing the technique of
weight functions
or
potential functions
that was first introduced in
[
24
]
and later explored in
[
7
]
,
[
4
]
and
[
3
]
.

We have yet to establish a connection between the ergodicity of either of the PCAs
G_{p,q,r}
and
\G_{p,q,r}
(respectively,
E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
and
\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
) and the event of draw in our generalized percolation game (respectively, bond percolation game). This is where
G_{p,q,r}
(respectively,
E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
) plays an important role.

Theorem 6
.

For each
(p,q,r)\in\Theta
, the generalized percolation game, with underlying parameters
p
,
q
and
r
, has probability
0
of resulting in a draw if and only if the PCA
G_{p,q,r}
is ergodic. Likewise, for each
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in\Theta^{\prime}
, the bond percolation game, with underlying parameters
r^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
, has probability
0
of resulting in a draw if and only if the PCA
E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
is ergodic.

Proof.

The proof of Theorem
6
happens via the exact same argument as the proof of Proposition 2.2 of
[
24
]
, and is therefore omitted from this paper.
∎

Our next result is Lemma
7.1
, and we note that it can be proved in exactly the same way as Proposition 2.1 of
[
24
]
(this lemma, in fact, gets used in proving one side of the two-way implication asserted upon in Theorem
6
):

Lemma 7.1
.

The PCA
\G_{p,q,r}
is ergodic if and only if the PCA
G_{p,q,r}
is ergodic. Likewise,
\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
is ergodic if and only if
E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
is ergodic.

Some discussion is now in order as to the philosophy behind invoking the notion of envelope PCAs. We let
F
denote a
d
-dimensional PCA endowed with the alphabet
\mathcal{A}_{F}=\{W,L\}
, some neighbourhood-marking set
\mathcal{N}_{F}
, and stochastic update rules given by some stochastic matrix
\varphi_{F}
. Introduced in
[
9
]
, the envelope PCA
\F
corresponding to
F
is a different PCA whose alphabet
\mathcal{A}_{\F}=\{W,L,D\}
is obtained by appending
\mathcal{A}_{F}
with the symbol
D
. The neighbourhood-marking set for
\F
is, once again,
\mathcal{N}_{F}
, and the stochastic update rules for
\F
are captured by the stochastic matrix
\varphi_{\F}
such that, when we restrict
\mathcal{A}_{\F}
to
\mathcal{A}_{F}
, the corresponding restriction of
\varphi_{\F}
yields
\varphi_{F}
. The state spaces for
F
and
\F
are respectively
\Omega_{F}=\mathcal{A}_{F}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and
\Omega_{\F}=\mathcal{A}_{\F}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
. A configuration
\eta=\left(\eta(\mathbf{x}):\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\right)
in
\Omega_{\F}
can be thought of as a configuration that actually belongs to
\Omega_{F}
, but with an unknown symbol (which could equal either
W
or
L
) occupying each coordinate
\mathbf{x}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for which
\eta(\mathbf{x})=D
. Thus, the symbol
D
acts as a placeholder whenever
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is occupied by a symbol from the original alphabet
\mathcal{A}_{F}
that we are not aware of or are uncertain about. Given an ergodic PCA
F
with the unique stationary distribution
\mu
, the motivation for introducing envelope PCAs lies in coming up with an efficient procedure, known as a
perfect sampling
procedure (see
[
29
]
and
[
9
]
), for generating a configuration from
\mu
. One such algorithm implements the
coupled from the past
(
CFTP
) method proposed in
[
29
]
, but it is inefficient when the state space
\Omega_{F}
is large (for instance, in this paper,
\Omega_{G_{p,q,r}}=\Omega_{E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}}=\{W,L\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is uncountably infinite). Even when the PCA
F
is ergodic, the corresponding envelope PCA
\F
may or may not be ergodic, but if it is, then
[
9
]
shows that an efficient perfect sampling procedure can be achieved by running
\F
on a single initial configuration. The envelope PCA
\F
is used to couple two (random) configurations obtained via (possibly repeated) applications of
F
, and as mentioned above, the symbol
D
is used to populate the cells of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
at which these two coupled configurations may differ from one another.

We are now in a position to prove Theorem
1
, assuming the conclusion of Theorem
4
, as follows:

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Let
(p,q,r)\in\Theta
satisfy the constraints stated in Theorem
1
. From Theorem
4
, we may conclude that
\mu\left[\left\{\boldsymbol{\eta}(x,k-x)=D\right\}\right]=0
for
any

x,k\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\mu
is the joint law of
(\boldsymbol{\eta}(x,k-x):x\in\mathbb{Z})
(see the first paragraph of §
7
). Such a conclusion is possible because we showed, just before stating Theorem
4
, that
\mu
is a translation-invariant and reflection-invariant stationary distribution for
\G_{p,q,r}
. In other words, for
any
vertex
(x,k-x)
lying on
any
diagonal
S_{k}
, the probability that it belongs to
D
equals
0
under
\mu
. Therefore, almost surely, there is
no
vertex in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
which, when serving as the initial vertex for the generalized percolation game, leads to a draw. This, along with Theorem
6
and Lemma
7.1
, yields the conclusion of Theorem
1
.
∎

The argument for proving Theorem
2
, provided Theorem
5
holds, is identical to the above.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

As explained above, once Theorem
4
has been established, the conclusion of Theorem
1
follows. By Theorem
6
, we then know that the PCA
G_{p,q,r}
is ergodic whenever the underlying parameter-triple
(p,q,r)
belongs to
\Theta
and satisfies the constraints stated in Theorem
1
. By Lemma
7.1
, we further conclude that the envelope PCA
\G_{p,q,r}
is ergodic for all such values of
(p,q,r)
as well. This concludes the proof of the first assertion stated in Theorem
3
. The proof of the second assertion of Theorem
3
follows via an identical argument (in which we replace Theorem
1
by Theorem
2
).
∎

All we have left to prove now are Theorems
4
and
5
.

8.
The proof of Theorem
4
by the technique of weight functions

It was stated in §
7
that the crux of this paper lies in the rather technical proof of Theorem
4
(and its counterpart in case of bond percolation games, Theorem
5
). Before proceeding with the proof, we summarize here, for the reader’s convenience, how the rest of §
8
has been organized:

(i)

In §
8.1
, we outline the central ideas relevant to the step-by-step construction of our weight function – these ideas apply equally well to the construction of the weight functions that allow us to prove Theorem
5
.

(ii)

In §
8.2
, we state, without proof, the weight function we come up with for the generalized percolation game, the
weight function inequality
it satisfies, and finally, draw the desired conclusion from this inequality (thus proving Theorem
4
) .

(iii)

In §
8.3
, we show the details of each step leading to the construction of our weight function.

8.1.
The principal ideas behind the construction of our weight functions

We present to the reader a very broad, but hopefully comprehensive, outline of how we aim to accomplish the proof of Theorem
4
. We denote by
\mathcal{R}
the subset of the parameter-space
\Theta
(defined in §
2
) that consists of all those triples
(p,q,r)
that satisfy the constraints stated in Theorem
1
– in other words,
p
,
q
and
r
are sufficiently small, the inequality in (
2.1
) holds, and precisely one of (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) is true. Although, in the rest of §
8.1
, we focus on
(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}
and work with the envelope PCA
\G_{p,q,r}
corresponding to generalized percolation games, this overview is equally pertinent to the construction of the weight functions corresponding to the three different regimes addressed in Theorem
5
for bond percolation games.

Recall, from §
3.2
, that the state space corresponding to our envelope PCA,
\G_{p,q,r}
, is
\Omega=\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
\hat{\mathcal{A}}=\{W,L,D\}
is its alphabet. Let
\mathcal{M}
denote the space of all reflection-invariant and translation-invariant (recall these definitions from §
7
) probability measures on
\Omega
, defined with respect to the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}
generated by all cylinder sets of
\Omega
. The goal is to come up with a “suitable” real-valued function
w
, referred to as a
weight function
or
potential function
, defined on
\mathcal{M}
. As such, a weight function (that serves the purpose outlined below) need not be unique, but as far as this paper is concerned, we construct
w
as a linear combination of the form

(8.1)

w(\mu)=\sum_{i=1}^{t}c_{i}\mu(\mathcal{C}_{i}),

where
t\in\mathbb{N}
, each of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\ldots
,
\mathcal{C}_{t}
is a cylinder set (thus belonging to
\mathcal{F}
), and each of
c_{1}
,
\ldots
,
c_{t}
is a real-valued function (in fact, a polynomial) in the parameters
p
,
q
and
r
. Our goal is to construct a weight function, of the form (
8.1
), that satisfies an inequality, henceforth referred to as a
weight function inequality
, of the form

(8.2)

w\left(\G_{p,q,r}\mu\right)\leqslant w(\mu)-\sum_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}c^{\prime}_{i}\mu\left(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i}\right),

where
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i}
is a cylinder set and
c^{\prime}_{i}
is a real-valued function (once again, a polynomial) in
p
,
q
and
r
, for each
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,t^{\prime}\}
. It must be ensured that

(8.3)

c^{\prime}_{i}\geqslant 0\text{ for each }i\in\{1,2,\ldots,t^{\prime}\}\text{ for each }(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}.

Let
\mathcal{I}
denote the set of all
i
, with
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,t^{\prime}\}
, such that
c^{\prime}_{i}>0
whenever
(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}
. If
\mu
is stationary for
\G_{p,q,r}
(so that
\G_{p,q,r}\mu=\mu
) and
\mathcal{I}
is non-empty, by (
8.3
) we conclude that
\mu\left(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i}\right)=0
for each
i\in\mathcal{I}
whenever
(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}
. If the cylinder sets
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{t^{\prime}}
have been chosen judiciously enough, suitable set-theoretic operations performed on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i}
, for
i\in\mathcal{I}
, allow us to conclude that
\mu\left((D)_{0}\right)=0
whenever
(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}
(here,
(D)_{0}
indicates the cylinder set that consists of
all
configurations
\eta=(\eta(x):x\in\mathbb{Z})
with
\eta(0)=D
, i.e. in which the
0
-th coordinate is occupied by the symbol
D
). This concludes the primary idea propelling the proof of Theorem
4
.

From here onward, we make use of some simplified notations, as follows. In its most general form, a cylinder set is indicated by
\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell}\right)_{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}}
, where
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in\hat{\mathcal{A}}
and
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}
, signifying that this cylinder set comprises all tuples
\eta=(\eta(x):x\in\mathbb{Z})
in which
\eta(x_{i})=a_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,\ell\}
. When
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}
are consecutive integers, i.e.
x_{i+1}=x_{i}+1
for each
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,\ell-1\}
, we have

\mu\left(\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell}\right)_{0,1,\ldots,\ell-1}\right)=\mu\left(\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell}\right)_{x_{1},x_{1}+1,\ldots,x_{1}+\ell-1}\right)

as
\mu
is translation-invariant. This allows us to abbreviate
\mu\left(\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell}\right)_{x_{1},x_{1}+1,\ldots,x_{1}+\ell-1}\right)
as simply
\mu\left(\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell}\right)\right)
for
any

x_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
. We further remove the commas and the outer parentheses and shorten
\mu\left(\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell}\right)\right)
to
\mu\left(a_{1}a_{2}\ldots a_{\ell}\right)
. We call a cylinder set, of the form
\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell}\right)_{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell}}
,
D
-inclusive
if
a_{i}=D
for at least one
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,\ell\}
.

We emphasize here that even proceeding via the approach outlined above, one may be able to come up with
different
weight functions all of which serve the same purpose (i.e. satisfy an inequality of the form (
8.2
), along with the criterion stated in (
8.3
)). Since the ultimate goal is to draw a conclusion about
\mu(D)
, it makes sense

(i)

to consider each cylinder set
\mathcal{C}_{i}
in (
8.1
) to be
D
-inclusive, and

(ii)

to begin our step-by-step construction of the weight function by setting
\mathcal{C}_{1}=(D)_{0}
in (
8.1
).

The subsequent choices for
\mathcal{C}_{2}
,
\mathcal{C}_{3}
etc. will be motivated in the subsubsections under §
8.3
. As seen in §
8.3
, in every step, up to and including the penultimate step, of constructing our weight function, we obtain a weight function inequality that, although of the same form as (
8.2
), fails to satisfy (
8.3
). This is what leads us to carry out an “adjustment” of the weight function obtained until that step. In §
8.1.2
, we present to the reader the heuristics of how such an adjustment is implemented, and how it contributes to satisfying a weight function inequality of the form (
8.2
). However, the implementation of this idea becomes much clearer when the reader goes through the specific details laid out in the various subsubsections of §
8.3
.

8.1.1.
How computations of the pushforward measures of various cylinder sets have been carried out in the sequel

Letting
\mathcal{C}=(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell})_{x_{1},x_{1}+1,\ldots,x_{1}+\ell-1}
be a cylinder set, we denote by
\mu(a_{0}\mathcal{C})
the probability of the cylinder set
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell})_{x_{1}-1,x_{1},x_{1}+1,\ldots,x_{1}+\ell-1}
, and by
\mu(\mathcal{C}a_{\ell+1})
the probability of the cylinder set
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}=(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell},a_{\ell+1})_{x_{1},x_{1}+1,\ldots,x_{1}+\ell-1,x_{1}+\ell}
. We now state the following lemma (whose proof is immediate and has been, therefore, omitted):

Lemma 8.1
.

For any cylinder set
\mathcal{C}=(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{\ell})_{x_{1},x_{1}+1,\ldots,x_{1}+\ell-1}
, and any probability measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
(i.e.
\mu
is translation-invariant, reflection-invariant and measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}
generated by all cylinder sets of
\Omega
), we can write

\mu(\mathcal{C})=\mu(W\mathcal{C})+\mu(D\mathcal{C})+\mu(L\mathcal{C})=\mu(\mathcal{C}W)+\mu(\mathcal{C}D)+\mu(\mathcal{C}L).

Lemma
8.1
has been used repeatedly in the computations of §
8.3
. In addition, another general result has been used, which is captured by Lemma
8.2
. Before stating Lemma
8.2
, we introduce the notation
\mathcal{C}\in\{W,L,D\}^{i}
, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, to mean that
\mathcal{C}
is a cylinder set of the form
(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1})_{0,1,\ldots,i-1}
. Given cylinder sets
\mathcal{C}=(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1})_{0,1,\ldots,i-1}
and
\mathcal{D}=(b_{0},b_{1},\ldots,b_{i})_{0,1,\ldots,i}
, we denote by
\Prob[\mathcal{C}\big|\mathcal{D}]
the probability of the event that
\G_{p,q,r}\eta(j)=a_{j}
for each
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,i-1\}
, conditioned on the event that
\eta(j)=b_{j}
for each
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,i\}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and each
\mathcal{C}\in\{W,L,D\}^{i}
,

\G_{p,q,r}\mu(\mathcal{C})=\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}^{i+1}}\Prob[\mathcal{C}\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D}).

When applying Lemma
8.2
, such as in the proofs of Lemmas
8.3
and
8.4
, we often simply write
\Prob[a_{0}a_{1}\ldots a_{i-1}\big|b_{0}b_{1}\ldots b_{i}]
in place of
\Prob[\mathcal{C}\big|\mathcal{D}]
.

8.1.2.
How each adjustment to the weight function has been carried out in the sequel

Let us denote by
w_{i-1}
the weight function we have constructed up until the start of the
i
-th adjustment (thus, the very first weight function we begin with is denoted by
w_{0}
, the weight function obtained after the first adjustment is denoted by
w_{1}
and so on). The weight function inequality at the
beginning
of the
i
-th adjustment will then be of the form

(8.4)

w_{i-1}\left(\G_{p,q,r}\mu\right)\leqslant w_{i-1}(\mu)-\sum_{t=1}^{k_{i-1}}\alpha_{i-1,t}\mu\left(\mathcal{C}_{i-1,t}\right),

for some
k_{i-1}\in\mathbb{N}
, coefficients
\alpha_{i-1,1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1,k_{i-1}}
that are polynomials in
p
,
q
and
r
, and cylinder sets
\mathcal{C}_{i-1,1},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{i-1,k_{i-1}}
. However, if there exists at least one coefficient among
\alpha_{i-1,1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1,k_{i-1}}
that is negative when
(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}
, the inequality (
8.4
) is unable to fulfill (
8.3
) when
(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}
, thereby necessitating an adjustment to the weight function
w_{i-1}
derived so far. We now define, for suitably chosen cylinder sets
\mathcal{D}_{i,1}
,
\ldots
,
\mathcal{D}_{i,\ell_{i}}
, and suitably chosen coefficients
\beta_{i,1}
,
\ldots
,
\beta_{i,\ell_{i}}
that are polynomials in
p
,
q
and
r
, the updated / adjusted weight function

(8.5)

w_{i}(\mu)=w_{i-1}(\mu)-\sum_{t=1}^{\ell_{i}}\beta_{i,t}\mu\left(\mathcal{D}_{i,t}\right).

When incorporated into (
8.4
),
w_{i}
yields the updated weight function inequality (obtained at the
end
of the
i
-th adjustment):

\displaystyle w_{i}\left(\G_{p,q,r}\mu\right)+\sum_{t=1}^{\ell_{i}}\beta_{i,t}\G_{p,q,r}\mu\left(\mathcal{D}_{i,t}\right)\leqslant w_{i}(\mu)+\sum_{t=1}^{\ell_{i}}\beta_{i,t}\mu\left(\mathcal{D}_{i,t}\right)-\sum_{t=1}^{k_{i-1}}\alpha_{i-1,t}\mu\left(\mathcal{C}_{i-1,t}\right)

(8.6)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

\displaystyle w_{i}\left(\G_{p,q,r}\mu\right)\leqslant w_{i}(\mu)+\sum_{t=1}^{\ell_{i}}\beta_{i,t}\mu\left(\mathcal{D}_{i,t}\right)-\sum_{t=1}^{k_{i-1}}\alpha_{i-1,t}\mu\left(\mathcal{C}_{i-1,t}\right)-\sum_{t=1}^{\ell_{i}}\beta_{i,t}\G_{p,q,r}\mu\left(\mathcal{D}_{i,t}\right).

Attempt is usually made to select
\mathcal{D}_{i,1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{i,\ell_{i}}
as subsets of elements from the set

(8.7)

\left\{\mathcal{C}_{i-1,t}:1\leqslant t\leqslant k_{i-1}\text{ and }\alpha_{i-1,t}>0\right\},

with the added restriction that if
\mathcal{D}_{i,t}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{i-1,t^{\prime}}
for some
\mathcal{C}_{i-1,t^{\prime}}
belonging to the set in (
8.7
), then
\beta_{i,t}\leqslant\alpha_{i-1,t^{\prime}}
, so that the coefficient of
\mu\left(\mathcal{C}_{i-1,t^{\prime}}\right)
in the right side of (
8.6
) remains non-positive. Attempt is also made to select
\mathcal{D}_{i,1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{i,\ell_{i}}
in such a manner that terms from the sum
-\sum_{t=1}^{\ell_{i}}\beta_{i,t}\G_{p,q,r}\mu\left(\mathcal{D}_{i,t}\right)
are able to aid in negating terms of the form
-\alpha_{i-1,t}\mu\left(\mathcal{C}_{i-1,t}\right)
in which
\alpha_{i-1,t}<0
when
(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}
.

The above gives a broad overview of

(i)

why adjustments to the weight function, in several rounds, are required, and

(ii)

how they are usually accomplished.

The above idea is reiterated until the final weight function inequality is of the form given in (
8.2
) and satisfies the criterion in (
8.3
).

8.2.
The final weight function obtained for the generalized percolation game when
(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}

We state here, for the reader’s convenience, the final weight functions obtained, and the corresponding weight function inequalities, of the form (
8.2
) (and obeying the criterion stated in (
8.3
)), that these weight functions satisfy when
(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}
. The final weight function that aids us in proving Theorem
4
, and, in turn, Theorem
1
, is

(8.8)

w(\mu)=\mu(D)+\mu(WD)+\mu(LWD)

when (
2.2
) is true, and

\displaystyle w(\mu)={}

\displaystyle\mu(D)+\mu(WD)+\{4r-2(p+q)+(p+q)^{2}-6r^{2}-3r(p+2q)\}\mu(LD)+(1-p-q)(1-r)

\displaystyle(-2r+r^{2}+3pr+3qr+r^{3})\mu(LDW)+(1-p-q)(1-r)(-1+p+q+r^{2}+2qr)\mu(LDL)

\displaystyle-[2-(3+p+q+r-2q^{2}-2r^{2}-5qr+2pq+pr+3pqr+4q^{2}r+4qr^{2}+5pr^{2}-2p^{2}r

(8.9)

\displaystyle-5pqr^{2}-3p^{2}r^{2}-2q^{2}r^{2})(1-p-q)(1-r)]\mu(LWD)

when any one of (
2.3
), (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) is true. The weight function inequality that each of the two functions in (
8.8
) and (
8.9
) satisfies (and which is of the form (
8.2
)) is given by

\displaystyle w(\G_{p,q,r}\mu)\leqslant{}

\displaystyle w(\mu)-[2-(1-p-q)(1-r)\{2+2p+q+r-q^{2}-r^{2}-3qr+pq+2q^{2}r+2qr^{2}+pqr+

\displaystyle 2pr^{2}-p^{2}r-2pqr^{2}-p^{2}r^{2}-q^{2}r^{2}\}]\{\mu(WD)-\mu(WWWD)-\mu(DWDD)-\mu(LWD)\}

\displaystyle-\{1-(1+p+q+pq+pr-q^{2}-qr-p^{2}r+q^{2}r)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\}\mu(DD)

(8.10)

\displaystyle-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(WLD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD).

Let us now deduce, from (
8.10
), our desired conclusion, i.e. that
\mu(D)=0
whenever
\mu
is a translation-invariant and reflection-invariant stationary distribution for
\G_{p,q,r}
. To this end, we shall focus on the term involving
\mu(DD)
in (
8.10
). Expanding its coefficient, we obtain:

\displaystyle-1+(1+p+q+pq+pr-q^{2}-qr-p^{2}r+q^{2}r)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle-p^{3}r^{3}-p^{2}q\{1-r-r^{2}+r^{3}\}-p^{2}r\{2-r-2r^{2}-p\}-pq^{2}r(1-r^{2})-pr^{3}-2q^{2}r^{3}-q^{3}r\{1+r-r^{2}\}

(8.11)

\displaystyle-qr\{1-pr-2q-r^{2}-2qr\}-q^{2}(1-q)-p(p+q-r)-q^{2}-r^{2}.

Since (
2.1
) is assumed to hold no matter which of (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.4
) and (
2.5
)
(p,q,r)
satisfies, we have

\displaystyle 2(p+q)-(p+q)^{2}+3r(p+2q)\geqslant 4r-6r^{2}\Longleftrightarrow p+q-r\geqslant r\left(1-3r-\frac{3p}{2}-3q\right)+\frac{1}{2}(p+q)^{2},

and this lower bound on the right side of the inequality above is strictly positive as long as
p
,
q
and
r
are sufficiently small but at least one of them is strictly positive. Note, furthermore, that if
p=0
, the term
-p(p+q-r)
disappears from (
8.11
), but
-q^{2}-r^{2}
remains, and this is strictly negative whenever at least one of
q
and
r
is non-zero. All these observations show us that when
p
,
q
and
r
are sufficiently small and (
2.1
) holds, the expression in (
8.11
) is strictly negative as long as at least one of
p
,
q
and
r
is strictly positive (recall, from §
2
, that the condition
p+q+r>0
is included in the definition of the parameter-space
\Theta
). Consequently, when
\mu
is stationary for
\G_{p,q,r}
, we deduce, from (
8.10
) and the identity
\G_{p,q,r}\mu=\mu
, that
\mu(DD)=0
.

Since
\G_{p,q,r}\mu=\mu
for any stationary
\mu
, hence
\mu(DD)=0\implies\G_{p,q,r}\mu(DD)=0
. Setting
\mathcal{C}=(D,D)_{0,1}
, and considering
\mathcal{D}\in\{(W,D,W)_{0,1,2},(W,D,L)_{0,1,2},(L,D,L)_{0,1,2}\}
, an application of Lemma
8.2
yields the inequality

(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\{\mu(WDW)+2r\mu(WDL)+r^{2}\mu(LDL)\}\leqslant\G_{p,q,r}\mu(DD),

so that when
r>0
, we have

\G_{p,q,r}\mu(DD)=0\implies\mu(WDW)=\mu(WDL)=\mu(LDL)=0.

Moreover, we have
\mu(WDD)=\mu(LDD)=\mu(DDD)=0
since each of them is bounded above by
\mu(DD)
(follows from Lemma
8.1
), and
\mu(DD)=0
. Since
\mu(D)=\mu(WDW)+\mu(LDL)+\mu(DDD)+2\mu(WDL)+2\mu(WDD)+2\mu(LDD)
(by Lemma
8.1
and the reflection invariance of
\mu
), we have
\mu(D)=0
, as desired.

Note that, when
r=0
, we are back to the set-up considered in
[
24
]
, and it has already been established there that the associated PCA,
\G_{p,q,0}
, is ergodic whenever
p+q>0
.

8.3.
Detailed construction of the weight function for generalized percolation games when
(p,q,r)\in\mathcal{R}

Recall that
\mathcal{R}\subset\Theta
comprises all those values of
(p,q,r)
that satisfy the constraints stated in Theorem
1
, i.e.
p
,
q
and
r
are sufficiently small, the inequality in (
2.1
) holds, and precisely one of (
2.2
), (
2.3
), (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) is true. Throughout §
8.3
, it is assumed that the probability measure
\mu
that we deal with is an element of
\mathcal{M}
, i.e. it is defined on the state space
\Omega=\hat{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathbb{Z}}=\{W,L,D\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
of
\G_{p,q,r}
, with respect to the
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{F}
generated by all cylinder sets of
\Omega
, and that
\mu
is both reflection-invariant and translation-invariant. Recall, from the discussion in the paragraph preceding §
8.1.2
, that beginning the construction of our weight function with the cylinder set
\mathcal{C}_{1}=(D)_{0}
seems to be a sensible and natural choice. Since, at each step, we must obtain an inequality of the form given by (
8.2
), it is crucial that we write down the pushforward measure
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(\mathcal{C}_{i})
for each cylinder set
\mathcal{C}_{i}
that we incorporate into our gradually-unfolding weight function. Let us, therefore, begin with the following lemma, which captures
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(\mathcal{C}_{1})
for
\mathcal{C}_{1}=(D)_{0}
:

Lemma 8.3
.

For each
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
, we have

(8.12)

\G_{p,q,r}\mu(D)=2(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WD)+2(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LD)+(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(DD).

Proof.

We apply Lemma
8.2
to the cylinder set
(D)_{1}
, obtain the conditional probabilities
\Prob[D\big|WD],\ldots,\Prob[D\big|DD]
from (
3.5
), (
3.6
) and (
3.7
), and use of the assumption that
\mu
is reflection-invariant, to obtain:

\displaystyle\G_{p,q,r}\mu(D)={}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\left\{\mu(WD)+\mu(DW)\right\}+(1-p-q)r(1-r)\left\{\mu(LD)+\mu(DL)\right\}

\displaystyle+(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(DD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WD)+2(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LD)+(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(DD),

as claimed.
∎

We now explain the intuition behind our choice of the second cylinder set,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, as we attempt to shape up our weight function in the form given by (
8.1
). The coefficient
2(1-p-q)r(1-r)
of
\mu(LD)
in (
8.12
) is always bounded above by
1
(in fact, by
1/2
), since
r(1-r)\leqslant 1/4
and
(1-p-q)\leqslant 1
. The coefficient
(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})
of
\mu(DD)
in (
8.12
) is obviously bounded above by
1
. On the other hand, when
p
,
q
and
r
are all sufficiently small, the coefficient
2(1-p-q)(1-r)
of
\mu(WD)
in (
8.12
) is very close to
2
. We can thus write, using (
8.12
) and the fact that
\mu(D)=\mu(WD)+\mu(LD)+\mu(DD)
, the following inequality:
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(D)\leqslant\mu(D)+\mu(WD)
. Comparing this with (
8.2
), it becomes evident that if
c_{1}=1
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}=(D)_{0}
in (
8.1
), a sensible choice for our next cylinder set and the corresponding coefficient would be
\mathcal{C}_{2}=(W,D)_{0,1}
and
c_{2}=1
. This now requires us to find the pushforward measure
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(WD)
:

Lemma 8.4
.

For any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
, we have

\displaystyle\G_{p,q,r}\mu(WD)={}

\displaystyle 2p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WD)+p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DD)+(1-p-q+r+pr-qr

\displaystyle-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3})(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LD)-r(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LDW)

\displaystyle-(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(LDL)+(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LWD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}

(8.13)

\displaystyle\mu(WLD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD).

Proof.

We apply Lemma
8.2
, keeping in mind that the only way the event
\{\G_{p,q,r}\eta(1)=D\}
happens with positive probability is if at least one of
\eta(1)
and
\eta(2)
equals
D
. For
\mathcal{D}=(b_{0},b_{1},b_{2})_{0,1,2}\in\{W,L,D\}^{3}
, computing the conditional probabilities

\Prob[WD\big|\mathcal{D}]=\Prob\left[\G_{p,q,r}\eta(0)=W\big|\eta(0)=b_{0},\eta(1)=b_{1}\right]\Prob\left[\G_{p,q,r}\eta(1)=D\big|\eta(1)=b_{1},\eta(2)=b_{2}\right]

by making use of (
3.2
) through (
3.7
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\G_{p,q,r}\mu(WD)={}

\displaystyle p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WDW)+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DDW)+\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LDW)+p(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(WDD)+p(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(DDD)

\displaystyle+\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(LDD)+p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WDL)

\displaystyle+p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(DDL)+\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LDL)

\displaystyle+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WWD)+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DWD)+\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LWD)+\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WLD)+

\displaystyle\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(DLD)+\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\}(1-p-q)

\displaystyle r(1-r)\mu(LLD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WDW)+p(1-p-q)(1-r)(2+r)\mu(DDW)+\{1-q-r+qr+2pr\}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LDW)+p(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(DDD)+\{1-q-r^{2}+2pr+qr^{2}+pr^{2}\}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DDL)+(1-q-r+pr+qr)(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LDL)

\displaystyle+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WWD)+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DWD)+(1-q-r+pr+qr)

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LWD)+(1-q-r+pr+qr)(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WLD)+

\displaystyle(1-q-r+pr+qr)(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(DLD)+(1-q-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2})(1-p-q)

\displaystyle r(1-r)\mu(LLD)\quad(\text{using reflection-invariance})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\underbrace{p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WDW)+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DDW)}_{(1)}

\displaystyle\underbrace{+p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(WDD)}_{(2)}\underbrace{+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LDW)}_{(1)}

\displaystyle\underbrace{+(1-p-q-r+qr+2pr)(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LDW)}_{(3)}\underbrace{+p(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(DDD)}_{(2)}

\displaystyle\underbrace{+p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(LDD)}_{(2)}\underbrace{+(1-p-q-r^{2}+pr+pr^{2}+qr^{2})(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LDD)}_{(3)}

\displaystyle\underbrace{+(1-q-r+pr+qr)(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LDL)}_{(3)}\underbrace{+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WWD)}_{(4)}

\displaystyle\underbrace{+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DWD)+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LWD)}_{(4)}+(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LWD)

\displaystyle\underbrace{+(1-q-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2})(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WLD)}_{(5)}-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(WLD)

\displaystyle\underbrace{+(1-q-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2})(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(DLD)}_{(5)}-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD)

\displaystyle\underbrace{+(1-q-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2})(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LLD)}_{(5)}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\underbrace{p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DW)}_{\text{combining terms underbraced by }(1)}\underbrace{+p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DD)}_{\text{combining terms underbraced by }(2)}

\displaystyle\underbrace{+(1-p-q-r^{2}+pr+pr^{2}+qr^{2})(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LD)}_{\text{combining terms underbraced by }(3)}-r(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LDW)

\displaystyle-(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(LDL)\underbrace{+p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WD)}_{\text{combining terms underbraced by }(4)}+(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LWD)

\displaystyle\underbrace{+(1-q-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2})(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LD)}_{\text{combining terms underbraced by }(5)}-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(WLD)

\displaystyle-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WD)+p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DD)+(1-p-q+r+pr-qr

\displaystyle-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3})(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LD)-r(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LDW)

\displaystyle-(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(LDL)+(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LWD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}

\displaystyle\mu(WLD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD),

where, in the last step, we combine terms using the reflection-invariance property of
\mu
.
∎

Recall that, so far, we have set
c_{1}=c_{2}=1
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}=(D)_{0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}=(W,D)_{0,1}
. The weight function, so far, equals
\mu(D)+\mu(WD)
, and the right side of the weight function inequality (of the form (
8.2
)) is given by

\displaystyle\G_{p,q,r}\mu(D)+\G_{p,q,r}\mu(WD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WD)+2(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LD)+(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(DD)

\displaystyle+2p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WD)+p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DD)+(1-p-q+r+pr-qr

\displaystyle-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3})(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LD)-r(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LDW)

\displaystyle-(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(LDL)+(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LWD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}

\displaystyle\mu(WLD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WD)+(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DD)+(1-p-q+3r

\displaystyle+pr-qr-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3})(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LD)-r(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LDW)

\displaystyle-(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(LDL)+(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LWD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}

(8.14)

\displaystyle\mu(WLD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD).

We now pause and focus on the coefficient of
\mu(LD)
in (
8.14
) (we need not focus on the coefficients of
\mu(WD)
and
\mu(DD)
since we can readily see that the former is bounded above by
2
and the latter by
1
). Writing

\displaystyle pr-qr-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3}=pr-qr-r^{2}(1-p-q)(1+r),

we see that the coefficient of
\mu(LD)
in (
8.14
) is given by

\displaystyle 1+2r-2(p+q)+(p+q)^{2}-3r^{2}-(p+q)r+3(p+q)r^{2}-(p+q)^{2}r

\displaystyle+pr(1-p-q)(1-r)-qr(1-p-q)(1-r)-r^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 1+2r-2(p+q)+(p+q)^{2}-3r^{2}-2qr+pr\{-1+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}

\displaystyle+r^{2}\{-(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}+3p+4q-pq-q^{2}\}-p^{2}r-pqr

(8.15)

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle 1+2r-2(p+q)+(p+q)^{2}-3r^{2}-2qr\quad\text{when }p,q,r\text{ are sufficiently small,}

and when (
2.1
) holds, we have

\displaystyle 2(p+q)-(p+q)^{2}-2r+3r^{2}+2qr\geqslant 4r-6r^{2}-3pr-6qr-2r+3r^{2}+2qr=r(2-3p-4q-3r)\geqslant 0

for all
p
and
r
sufficiently small, thus proving that the expression in (
8.15
), and consequently, the coefficient of
\mu(LD)
in (
8.14
), is bounded above by
1
.

We now continue with (
8.14
), and write (decomposing
\mu(D)
as in Lemma
8.1
):

\displaystyle\G_{p,q,r}\mu(D)+\G_{p,q,r}\mu(WD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mu(D)+\mu(WD)-2\{1-(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\}\mu(WD)-\{1-(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\}\mu(DD)

\displaystyle-\{1-(1-p-q+3r+pr-qr-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3})(1-p-q)(1-r)\}\mu(LD)

\displaystyle-r(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LDW)-(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(LDL)+(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LWD)

(8.16)

\displaystyle-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(WLD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD),

and given the observations made above regarding the coefficient of
\mu(LD)
in (
8.14
), we conclude that, when
p
,
q
and
r
are all sufficiently small and (
2.1
) holds, the only term on the right side of (
8.16
), other than
\mu(D)
and
\mu(WD)
, that has a non-negative coefficient is
(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LWD)
. Moreover, when
p
,
q
and
r
are all sufficiently small, we have
(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}=\Theta(1)
(i.e. its order of magnitude is the same as the constant
1
when
p
,
q
and
r
approach
0
). Hence, it seems sensible to set
\mathcal{C}_{3}=(L,W,D)_{0,1,2}
and
c_{3}=1
, and the weight function obtained thus far becomes

(8.17)

w_{0}(\mu)=\mu(D)+\mu(WD)+\mu(LWD).

This necessitates the computation of
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LWD)
, which has been accomplished via Lemma
8.5
.

Lemma 8.5
.

For any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
, we can write
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LWD)=\sum_{i=1}^{9}A_{i}
, where

(8.18)

\displaystyle A_{1}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle(1-p)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LD),

(8.19)

\displaystyle A_{2}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DD),

(8.20)

\displaystyle A_{3}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\{1+p-q-r+2pr+qr\}\mu(LWD),,

(8.21)

\displaystyle A_{4}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LDL),,

(8.22)

\displaystyle A_{5}={}

\displaystyle(1-p)p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WDL),,

\displaystyle A_{6}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DW)

(8.23)

\displaystyle+\{p(1-p)-(q+r-pr-qr)(1-q-r+pr+qr)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WWDW),

(8.24)

\displaystyle A_{7}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle p(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(WWWD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WD),

(8.25)

\displaystyle A_{8}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle(q+r-pr-qr)(1-q-r+pr+qr)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(LDD),

\displaystyle A_{9}\leqslant{}

\displaystyle q(1-q-r+pr+qr)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(WDD)

\displaystyle+\{p(1-p)-q(1-q-r+pr+qr)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(WWDD)

(8.26)

\displaystyle+(pr+qr-q)(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DWDD).

Proof.

Extending the notation introduced just before stating Lemma
8.2
, we now let
R_{0}\times R_{1}\times\cdots\times R_{i-1}
, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
R_{j}\subset\{W,L,D\}
for each
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,i-1\}
, denote the set of all cylinder sets
(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1})_{0,1,\ldots,i-1}
such that
a_{j}\in R_{j}
for each
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,i-1\}
. Applying Lemma
8.2
to the cylinder set
\mathcal{C}=(L,W,D)_{0,1,2}
, and keeping in mind that in order for the event
\{\G_{p,q,r}\eta(2)=D\}
to take place with positive probability, at least one of the events
\{\eta(2)=D\}
and
\{\eta(3)=D\}
must occur, we can write:

\displaystyle\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LWD)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}^{4}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D})=\sum_{i=1}^{9}A_{i},

where we set

\displaystyle A_{1}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}^{2}\times\{L\}\times\{D\}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D}),

\displaystyle A_{2}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}\times\{D\}^{2}\times\{W,L,D\}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D}),

\displaystyle A_{3}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}\times\{L\}\times\{W\}\times\{D\}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D})+\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{L\}\times\{W\}\times\{D\}\times\{W,L,D\}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D}),

\displaystyle A_{4}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}\times\{L\}\times\{D\}\times\{L\}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D}),

\displaystyle A_{5}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,D\}\times\{W\}\times\{D\}\times\{L\}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D}),

\displaystyle A_{6}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}\times\{L\}\times\{D\}\times\{W\}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D})+\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,D\}\times\{W\}\times\{D\}\times\{W\}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D}),

\displaystyle A_{7}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}\times\{W,D\}\times\{W\}\times\{D\}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D}),

\displaystyle A_{8}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}\times\{L\}\times\{D\}^{2}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D}),

\displaystyle A_{9}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{D}\in\{W,D\}\times\{W\}\times\{D\}^{2}}\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]\mu(\mathcal{D}).

In the computation of
A_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,9\}
, we make use of the relation

\displaystyle\Prob[LWD\big|\mathcal{D}]={}

\displaystyle\Prob\left[\G_{p,q,r}\eta(0)=L\big|\eta(0)=b_{0},\eta(1)=b_{1}\right]\Prob\left[\G_{p,q,r}\eta(1)=W\big|\eta(1)=b_{1},\eta(2)=b_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\Prob\left[\G_{p,q,r}\eta(2)=D\big|\eta(2)=b_{2},\eta(3)=b_{3}\right],

where
\mathcal{D}=(b_{0},b_{1},b_{2},b_{3})_{0,1,2,3}
, and we apply the stochastic update rules of (
3.2
) through (
3.7
).

We begin with

\displaystyle A_{1}={}

\displaystyle(1-p)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WWLD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)

\displaystyle(1-r^{2})\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WLLD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r

\displaystyle(1-r)\mu(LWLD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LLLD)+

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WDLD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}

\displaystyle\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(DWLD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LDLD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)

(8.27)

\displaystyle\mu(DLLD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(DDLD).

A quick comparison of the coefficients in the various terms of (
8.27
) reveals that the coefficient of each of
\mu(LWLD)
,
\mu(LLLD)
,
\ldots
,
\mu(DDLD)
is bounded above by the coefficient of
\mu(WLLD)
, and

\displaystyle(1-p)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}-\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-p)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}-\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)+r(1-p-q)(1-r)\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}-\{q+(1-p-q)r\}r(1-p-q)(1-r)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r-r^{2})-qr\}

is non-negative whenever
p
,
q
and
r
are all sufficiently small, so that the coefficient of
\mu(WWLD)
exceeds that of
\mu(WLLD)
in (
8.27
). For each cylinder set
\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}^{2}\times\{L\}\times\{D\}
, let us denote the corresponding coefficient by
\alpha_{\mathcal{D}}

(
for instance, the coefficient of
\mu(DLLD)
is
\alpha_{DLLD}=\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)
)
, and let us define

\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}=\alpha_{\mathcal{D}}-\alpha_{WWLD}=\alpha_{\mathcal{D}}-(1-p)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)

for each cylinder set
\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}^{2}\times\{L\}\times\{D\}\setminus\left\{(W,W,L,D)_{0,1,2,3}\right\}
. From our discussion above, it is evident that when
p
,
q
and
r
are all small enough, each
\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}\leqslant 0
, so that we may rewrite (
8.27
) as

\displaystyle A_{1}={}

\displaystyle(1-p)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{WLLD}\mu(WLLD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{LWLD}\mu(LWLD)

\displaystyle+\alpha^{\prime}_{LLLD}\mu(LLLD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{WDLD}\mu(WDLD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{DWLD}\mu(DWLD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{LDLD}\mu(LDLD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{DLLD}\mu(DLLD)

\displaystyle+\alpha^{\prime}_{DDLD}\mu(DDLD)\leqslant(1-p)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LD),

which proves the inequality in (
8.18
). Next, we have

\displaystyle A_{2}={}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WDDW)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DDDW)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LDDW)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(WDDD)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}p(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(DDDD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}p(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(LDDD)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WDDL)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(DDDL)

(8.28)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LDDL).

A quick comparison of the coefficients of the various terms in (
8.28
) reveals that the coefficient of
\mu(WDDD)
, namely,
\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)
, is the largest. As in the analysis of
A_{1}
, letting
\alpha_{\mathcal{D}}
denote the coefficient of
\mu(\mathcal{D})
in (
8.28
) for every
\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}\times\{D\}^{2}\times\{W,L,D\}
, and setting

\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}=\alpha_{\mathcal{D}}-\alpha_{WDDD}=\alpha_{\mathcal{D}}-\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)

for each
\mathcal{D}\in\{W,L,D\}\times\{D\}^{2}\times\{W,L,D\}\setminus\left\{(W,D,D,D)_{0,1,2,3}\right\}
, we know, from our observation above, that each
\alpha^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}\leqslant 0
, and we can rewrite (
8.28
) as:

\displaystyle A_{2}={}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{WDDW}\mu(WDDW)+\alpha^{\prime}_{DDDW}\mu(DDDW)

\displaystyle+\alpha^{\prime}_{LDDW}\mu(LDDW)+\alpha^{\prime}_{DDDD}\mu(DDDD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{LDDD}\mu(LDDD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{WDDL}\mu(WDDL)+\alpha^{\prime}_{DDDL}\mu(DDDL)

\displaystyle+\alpha^{\prime}_{LDDL}\mu(LDDL)\leqslant\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DD),

which proves the bound in (
8.19
). From the definition of
A_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle A_{3}={}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WLWD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}

\displaystyle\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DLWD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LLWD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LWDW)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}

\displaystyle p(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(LWDD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LWDL)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LWD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{DLWD}\mu(DLWD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{LLWD}

\displaystyle\mu(LLWD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r^{2})\mu(LWD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{LWDW}\mu(LWDW)+\alpha^{\prime}_{LWDL}\mu(LWDL)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\{1+p-q-r+2pr+qr\}\mu(LWD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{DLWD}\mu(DLWD)

(8.29)

\displaystyle+\alpha^{\prime}_{LLWD}\mu(LLWD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{LWDW}\mu(LWDW)+\alpha^{\prime}_{LWDL}\mu(LWDL),

where each of the coefficients

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}_{DLWD}=\alpha^{\prime}_{LLWD}=-r(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\},

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}_{LWDW}=-r\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r),

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}_{LWDL}=-\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)

is non-positive, letting (
8.29
) imply the inequality in (
8.20
). Next, we have

\displaystyle A_{4}={}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WLDL)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(DLDL)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LLDL)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LDL)

(8.30)

\displaystyle+\alpha^{\prime}_{DLDL}\mu(DLDL)+\alpha^{\prime}_{LLDL}\mu(LLDL),

where
\alpha^{\prime}_{DLDL}=\alpha^{\prime}_{LLDL}=-\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)^{2}r^{2}(1-r)^{2}
are both non-positive coefficients, thus letting (
8.30
) imply that the inequality in (
8.21
) is true. Keeping in mind that we have already taken into account
\mathcal{D}=(L,W,D,L)_{0,1,2,3}
in
A_{3}
, we obtain, from the definition of
A_{5}
,

\displaystyle A_{5}={}

\displaystyle(1-p)p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WWDL)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(DWDL)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-p)p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WDL)-pr(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(DWDL)

\displaystyle-(1-p)p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LWDL)\leqslant(1-p)p(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(WDL),

proving (
8.22
). Keeping in mind that
\mathcal{D}=(L,W,D,W)_{0,1,2,3}
has already been taken into account in
A_{3}
, we obtain, from the definition of
A_{6}
:

\displaystyle A_{6}={}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WLDW)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DLDW)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LLDW)

\displaystyle+(1-p)p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WWDW)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DWDW)

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DW)+\alpha^{\prime}_{DLDW}\mu(DLDW)

\displaystyle+\alpha^{\prime}_{LLDW}\mu(LLDW)+\{p(1-p)-(q+r-pr-qr)(1-q-r+pr+qr)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)

(8.31)

\displaystyle\mu(WWDW)+\alpha^{\prime}_{DWDW}\mu(DWDW),

(the sum of
\mu(WLDW)
,
\mu(DLDW)
,
\mu(LLDW)
,
\mu(WWDW)
and
\mu(DWDW)
is bounded above by
\mu(DW)
, which follows from Lemma
8.1
) where each of

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}_{DLDW}=\alpha^{\prime}_{LLDW}=-r\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2},

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}_{DWDW}=-\{q+(1-p-q)r\}(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}

is a non-positive, allowing us to conclude, from (
8.31
), that (
8.23
) is true. Next, we observe that

\displaystyle A_{7}={}

\displaystyle(1-p)p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WWWD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DWWD)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LWWD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)

\displaystyle\mu(WDWD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(DDWD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}

\displaystyle p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(LDWD)

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle p(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(WWWD)+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WD)

(8.32)

\displaystyle+\alpha^{\prime}_{DDWD}\mu(DDWD)+\alpha^{\prime}_{LDWD}\mu(LDWD),

where
\alpha^{\prime}_{DDWD}=\alpha^{\prime}_{LDWD}=-pr(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}
(the last inequality follows from the fact that the sum of
\mu(WWWD)
,
\mu(DWWD)
,
\mu(LWWD)
,
\mu(WDWD)
,
\mu(DDWD)
and
\mu(LDWD)
is bounded above by
\mu(WD)
, which, in turn, follows from Lemma
8.1
), thus allowing (
8.32
) to imply that (
8.24
) holds. The second last term in the expansion of
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LWD)
equals

\displaystyle A_{8}={}

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(WLDD)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DLDD)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(LLDD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(q+r-pr-qr)(1-q-r+pr+qr)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(LDD)

\displaystyle-r\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1+r)\{\mu(DLDD)+\mu(LLDD)\},

corroborating the inequality in (
8.25
), whereas the final term in the expansion of
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LWD)
equals

\displaystyle A_{9}={}

\displaystyle(1-p)p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(WWDD)

(8.33)

\displaystyle+\{q+(1-p-q)r\}p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DWDD)

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle q(1-q-r+pr+qr)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(WDD)

\displaystyle+\{p(1-p)-q(1-q-r+pr+qr)\}(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(WWDD)

\displaystyle+(pr+qr-q)(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)(1+r)\mu(DWDD),

(the second step is obtained by first applying Lemma
8.1
, then ignoring the term involving
\mu(LWDD)
since its coefficient is non-positive), corrborating (
8.26
). This concludes the proof of Lemma
8.5
.
∎

The weight function inequality of the form given by (
8.2
), corresponding to the weight function in (
8.17
), can now be derived as follows, making use of (
8.16
) and Lemma
8.5
:

\displaystyle w_{0}(\G_{p,q,r}\mu)={}

\displaystyle\G_{p,q,r}\mu(D)+\G_{p,q,r}\mu(WD)+\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LWD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mu(D)+\mu(WD)-2\{1-(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\}\mu(WD)-\{1-(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)

\displaystyle(1+r)\}\mu(DD)-\{1-(1-p-q+3r+pr-qr-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3})(1-p-q)

\displaystyle(1-r)\}\mu(LD)-r(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LDW)-(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(LDL)+(1-p-q)^{2}

\displaystyle(1-r)^{2}\mu(LWD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(WLD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD)

\displaystyle+\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LWD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mu(D)+\mu(WD)+\mu(LWD)-2\{1-(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\}\mu(WD)

\displaystyle-\{1-(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\}\mu(DD)-\{1-(1-p-q+3r+pr-qr-r^{2}+pr^{2}

\displaystyle+qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3})(1-p-q)(1-r)\}\mu(LD)-r(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LDW)

\displaystyle-(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(LDL)-\{1-(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\}\mu(LWD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}

\displaystyle\mu(WLD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD)+\sum_{i=1}^{9}A_{i}

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle w_{0}(\mu)-2\{1-(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\}\mu(WD)-\{1-(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\}\mu(DD)

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\{1-(1-p-q+3r+pr-qr-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3})(1-p-q)(1-r)\}\mu(LD)}

\displaystyle-r(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\mu(LDW)-(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(LDL)-\{1-(1-p-q)^{2}(1-r)^{2}\}

\displaystyle\mu(LWD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(WLD)-r^{2}(1-r)^{2}(1-p-q)^{2}\mu(DLD)

(8.34)

\displaystyle\underbrace{+(1-p)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LD)}+\sum_{i=2}^{9}A_{i},

where the last step is obtained by implementing the inequality of (
8.18
). Combining the terms involving
\mu(LD)
(indicated by underbraces) in (
8.34
), we obtain:

\displaystyle-\{1-(1-p-q+3r+pr-qr-r^{2}+pr^{2}+qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3})(1-p-q)(1-r)\}\mu(LD)

\displaystyle+(1-p)\{p+(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p-q)r(1-r)\mu(LD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle[-1+(1-p-q+4r-2qr-2r^{2}+pqr+3pr^{2}+2qr^{2}-r^{3}+pr^{3}+qr^{3}-p^{2}r^{2}-pqr^{2})

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)]\mu(LD)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle[-1+\{1-p-q+4r-qr(2-p-2r)-r^{2}(2-3p)-r^{3}(1-p-q)-p^{2}r^{2}-pqr^{2}\}

(8.35)

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)]\mu(LD)

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle[-1+(1-p-q+4r)(1-p-q)(1-r)]\mu(LD)\quad\text{when }p,r\text{ are sufficiently small;}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle[-1+\{1+3r-4r^{2}-2(p+q)+(p+q)^{2}-(p+q)r-(p+q)r(1+p+q-4r)\}]\mu(LD)

(8.36)

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle\{3r-4r^{2}-2(p+q)+(p+q)^{2}-(p+q)r\}]\mu(LD)\quad\text{when }r\text{ sufficiently small}.

When
p
,
q
and
r
are sufficiently small and the inequality in (
2.1
) holds, we have

\displaystyle 2(p+q)-(p+q)^{2}-3r+4r^{2}+(p+q)r

\displaystyle\geqslant{}

\displaystyle 4r-6r^{2}-3pr-6qr-3r+4r^{2}+(p+q)r=r(1-2p-5q-2r)\geqslant 0,

so that the coefficient of
\mu(LD)
in (
8.36
) is non-positive, and consequently, the coefficient of
\mu(LD)
in (
8.35
), is non-positive. Incorporating (
8.35
) into (
8.34
), we obtain:

\displaystyle w_{0}(\G_{p,q,r}\mu)\leqslant{}

\displaystyle w_{0}(\mu)-2\{1-(1+p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\}\mu(WD)-\{1-(1+p)(1-p-q)

\displaystyle(q+r-pr-qr)(1-q-r+pr+qr)(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(LDD)

\displaystyle(1+p+q+pq+pr-q^{2}-qr-p^{2}r+q^{2}r)\text{ when }p,q,r\text{ are sufficiently small}.

Here, we provide a brief explanation as to why the first inequality of (
2.5
) ensures, when
p
,
q
and
r
are sufficiently small, that
p(1-p)>(q+r-pr-qr)(1-q-r+pr+qr)
, and this, in turn, implies that
p(1-p)>q(1-q-r+pr+qr)
. The function
f(x)=x(1-x)
, for
x\in[0,1]
, is strictly increasing on
[0,1/2)
and strictly decreasing on
(1/2,1]
. When
p
is sufficiently small (in particular,
p<1/2
), and the first inequality of (
2.5
) holds, we can, therefore, write

\frac{1}{2}>p>q+r(1-p-q)\implies f(p)>f(q+r(1-p-q))\Longleftrightarrow p(1-p)>(q+r-pr-qr)(1-q-r+pr+qr),

which explains the claim made in (
iii
), and since
q+r(1-p-q)\geqslant q
(since each of
r
and
(1-p-q)
is non-negative), the inequality above further implies that
p(1-p)>q(1-q-r+pr+qr)
, which explains the claim stated in (
iv
).

At this point, our first attempt is to try to negate, to as large an extent as possible, the terms involving
\mu(WWWD)

in (

) of Theorem

Here, we assume that

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\{p-q-r-3p^{2}-3q^{2}-3r^{2}-4pq-3pr-5qr+4p^{2}r+6pr^{2}+6q^{2}r+6qr^{2}+10pqr\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\{p(1-p)-q-r-2p^{2}-3q^{2}-3r^{2}-4pq-3pr-5qr+4p^{2}r+6pr^{2}+6q^{2}r+6qr^{2}

\displaystyle+10pqr\}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\{q-q^{2}-qr(1-p-q)-q-r-2p^{2}-3q^{2}-3r^{2}-4pq-3pr-5qr+4p^{2}r+6pr^{2}

\displaystyle+6q^{2}r+6qr^{2}+10pqr\}

which is non-positive for all

satisfies (

Since the coefficient of each of
\mu(LD)

\mu(WWDW)
,
\mu(WWDD)
and

by

8.3.2.
When either (

The reason for considering these two possibilities together is that the way we proceed, to a large extent, is the same for both of them (in fact, much of this approach is also common to the scenario where

The very first observation to make is that the coefficient of

iii
), that the inequality
p(1-p)\leqslant\{q+r(1-p-q)\}\{1-q-r(1-p-q)\}
, which is a part of both (
2.3
) and (
2.4
), becomes equivalent to
p\leqslant q+r(1-p-q)
when
p\leqslant 1/2
and
q+r(1-p-q)\leqslant 1/2
(in other words, when
p
,
q
and
r
are sufficiently small). Applying the latter inequality, the coefficient of
\mu(WWWD)
in (
8.64
) can be bounded above as follows:

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\{p-q-r-3p^{2}-3q^{2}-3r^{2}-4pq-3pr-5qr+4p^{2}r+6pr^{2}+6q^{2}r+6qr^{2}+10pqr\}

\displaystyle\leqslant{}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\{q+r(1-p-q)-q-r-3p^{2}-3q^{2}-3r^{2}-4pq-3pr-5qr+4p^{2}r+6pr^{2}+6q^{2}r

\displaystyle+6qr^{2}+10pqr\}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\{-3p^{2}-3q^{2}-3r^{2}-4pq-4pr-6qr+4p^{2}r+6pr^{2}+6q^{2}r+6qr^{2}+10pqr\}

(8.65)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\{-3p^{2}-3q^{2}-3r^{2}-2pq(2-5r)-2pr(2-2p-3r)-6qr(1-q-r)\},

which is non-positive for all
p
,
q
and
r
sufficiently small. This shows us that the coefficient of
\mu(WWWD)
in (
8.64
) is non-positive in this case. Only the coefficient of
\mu(WWDD)
in (
8.64
) remains non-negative.

This is where the need for an adjustment to our weight function, along the lines of the idea outlined in §
8.1.2
, arises. There are quite a few terms with non-positive coefficients available to us on the right side of (
8.64
), but we should take into account the following aspects while deciding which of them to use for negating the existing (possibly) non-negative terms on the right side of (
8.64
):

(i)

which of the cylinder sets (namely,
(D,D)_{0,1}
,
(L,D)_{0,1}
,
(L,D,W)_{0,1,2}
etc.) appearing on the right side of (
8.64
), with non-positive coefficients, can boast a significant contribution from
\mu(WWD)
,
\mu(WWWD)
,
\mu(WWDW)
and
\mu(WWDD)
when we consider their probabilities under the pushforward measure (i.e. when we consider the expansion of
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(DD)
,
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LD)
,
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LDW)
etc.);

(ii)

the order of magnitude of the coefficient of each of these terms assuming
p
,
q
and
r
to be sufficiently small (for instance, the coefficient of
\mu(LDW)
in (
8.64
) is of the order of
r
, that of
\mu(LDL)
is of the order of
1
, and so on).

To this end, we make use of the following lemma:

Lemma 8.6
.

For any
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
, the following inequalities are true:

(8.66)

\displaystyle(1-p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WWD)\leqslant\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LD),

(8.67)

\displaystyle(1-p)(1-p-q)(1-r)p\{\mu(WWDW)+\mu(WWDD)\}\leqslant\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LDW),

\displaystyle(1-p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\{q+(1-p-q)r\}\mu(WWDW)+(1-p)(1-p-q)(1-r)

(8.68)

\displaystyle\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}\mu(WWDD)\leqslant\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LDL),

\displaystyle(1-p)p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WWDW)+(1-p)p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)\mu(WWDD)

(8.69)

\displaystyle+(1-p)p(1-p-q)(1-r)\mu(WWWD)\leqslant\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LWD).

Proof.

The proof of Lemma
8.6
follows from Lemma
8.2
and the stochastic update rules given by (
3.2
) through (
3.7
). The reason why we obtain inequalities instead of equalities is because, when computing
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(\mathcal{C})
where
\mathcal{C}
is any of the cylinder sets
(L,D)_{0,1}
,
(L,D,W)_{0,1,2}
,
(L,D,L)_{0,1,2}
and
(L,W,D)_{0,1,2}
, we do not consider
all
possible cylinder sets
\mathcal{D}
for which
\Prob[\mathcal{C}\big|\mathcal{D}]>0
. The details of the proof are as follows:

(i)

For the lower bound on
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LD)
, we focus on the cylinder set
\mathcal{C}=(L,D)_{0,1}
, and we consider
\mathcal{D}=(W,W,D)_{0,1,2}
. Noting that
\Prob[\mathcal{C}\big|\mathcal{D}]
equals
(1-p)(1-p-q)(1-r)
, we now obtain (
8.66
) as a consequence of Lemma
8.2
.

(ii)

For the lower bound on
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LDW)
, we focus on the cylinder set
\mathcal{C}=(L,D,W)_{0,1,2}
, and we consider
\mathcal{D}\in\{W\}^{2}\times\{D\}\times\{W,D\}
, and noting that
\Prob[\mathcal{C}\big|\mathcal{D}]
equals
(1-p)(1-p-q)(1-r)p
for each
\mathcal{D}
here, we obtain (
8.67
) as a consequence of Lemma
8.2
.

(iii)

For the lower bound on
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LDL)
, we focus on the cylinder set
\mathcal{C}=(L,D,L)_{0,1,2}
, and we consider
\mathcal{D}\in\{W\}^{2}\times\{D\}\times\{W,D\}
. Noting that
\Prob[\mathcal{C}\big|\mathcal{D}]
equals
(1-p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\{q+(1-p-q)r\}
when
\mathcal{D}=(W,W,D,W)_{0,1,2,3}
, whereas it equals
(1-p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\{q+(1-p-q)r^{2}\}
when
\mathcal{D}=(W,W,D,D)_{0,1,2,3}
, we obtain (
8.68
) as a consequence of Lemma
8.2
.

(iv)

Finally, for the lower bound on
\G_{p,q,r}\mu(LWD)
, we focus on the cylinder set
\mathcal{C}=(L,W,D)_{0,1,2}
, and we consider
\mathcal{D}\in\left(\{W\}^{2}\times\{D\}\times\{W,D\}\right)\cup\left(\{W\}^{3}\times\{D\}\right)
. Noting that
\Prob[\mathcal{C}\big|\mathcal{D}]
equals
(1-p)p(1-p-q)(1-r)
when
\mathcal{D}\in\left\{(W,W,D,W)_{0,1,2,3},(W,W,W,D)_{0,1,2,3}\right\}
, and it equals
(1-p)p(1-p-q)(1-r)(1+r)
when
\mathcal{D}=(W,W,D,D)_{0,1,2,3}
, we obtain (
8.69
) as a consequence of Lemma
8.2
.∎

\displaystyle+3qr+r^{3})\mu(LDW)+(1-p-q)(1-r)(-1+p+q+r^{2}+2qr)\mu(LDL)-[3-(3+p+q+r

\displaystyle-2q^{2}-2r^{2}-5qr+2pq+pr+3pqr+4q^{2}r+4qr^{2}+5pr^{2}-2p^{2}r-5pqr^{2}-3p^{2}r^{2}-2q^{2}r^{2})

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)]\mu(LWD).

8.3.3.
When
(p,q,r)
satisfies (
2.5
) of Theorem
1

The hardest case to consider is where (
2.5
) is true. Most of the approach adopted so far also applies to this case, i.e. the weight function is updated as in (
8.70
), the coefficient of
\mu(WWDD)
is updated to the expression given by (

), and the weight function inequality is updated to (

). Observe that in the regime given by (

), there is no guarantee that the coefficient of any of
\mu(WWDW)
,
\mu(WWDD)
and
\mu(WWWD)
in (

) is non-positive.

\displaystyle+3qr+r^{3})(1-p)p+(-1+p+q+r^{2}+2qr)(1-p)(1-p-q)(1-r)\{q+(1-p-q)r\}-\{3-(3+p+

\displaystyle(1-p-q)(1-r)\}(1-p)p]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle[-p^{5}r(2+r-3r^{2})-p^{4}qr^{2}(9-8r)-p^{4}qr-11p^{4}r^{3}-7p^{3}q^{2}r^{2}(2-r)-20p^{3}qr^{3}-p^{3}qr(4-25r-7q)

) (once we replace the notation

), and (

) is of the same form as (

) and satisfies (

). This completes the construction of our weight function (the final form of which is as given by (

)), for the generalized percolation games, when the underlying parameter-triple
(p,q,r)
satisfies the constraints given by (
2.5
) of Theorem
1
.

9.
The proof of Theorem
5
by the technique of weight functions

The principal ideas involved in constructing our weight function for each of the three regimes stated in Theorem
2
are the same as those outlined in §
8.1
(including, as outlined in §
8.1.2
, the ideas implemented for the iterative tweaking of the weight function until it satisfies an inequality of the form given by (
8.2
), along with the criterion stated in (
8.3
)). However, as becomes evident when one goes through the steps leading to the construction of the weight function for the proof of Theorem
4
, outlined in §
8.3
, the details involved are rather
ad hoc
and
specific
to the regime of values of the parameter-pair
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})
under consideration. Consequently, we dedicate §
9.2
to the detailed construction of the weight function for the regime given by
(B1)
, §
9.3
to that for the regime given by
(B2)
, and §
9.4
to that for the regime given by
(B3)
.

9.1.
The final weight functions obtained for the bond percolation game when
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})
belongs to one of the regimes given by
(B1)
,
(B2)
and
(B3)

As in §
8.2
, we state here, for the reader’s convenience, the final weight function we have come up with, and the corresponding weight function inequality it satisfies, for
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})
belonging to each of the regimes given by
(B1)
,
(B2)
and
(B3)
. When
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})
satisfies the constraints of
(B1)
, the weight function is given by

\displaystyle w(\mu)={}

\displaystyle\mu(D)+\mu(WD)+\Big[1-(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})\Big\{2(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})(1-s^{\prime})+r^{\prime}+(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})^{2}(1-s^{\prime})+\frac{2{r^{\prime}}^{2}}{(1-s^{\prime})}

\displaystyle-(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})^{2}r^{\prime}-\frac{5(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2}){r^{\prime}}^{2}}{(1-s^{\prime})}\Big\}\Big]\mu(LWD)-(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})\Big\{-2(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})(1-s^{\prime})-4r^{\prime}

\displaystyle+(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})^{2}(1-s^{\prime})+\frac{3{r^{\prime}}^{2}}{(1-s^{\prime})}+2(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})r^{\prime}-2(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})^{2}r^{\prime}-\frac{6(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2}){r^{\prime}}^{2}}{(1-s^{\prime})}\Big\}\mu(LD)-

\displaystyle(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})\Big\{3r^{\prime}+2(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})^{2}(1-s^{\prime})+\frac{2{r^{\prime}}^{2}}{(1-s^{\prime})}-(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})r^{\prime}-2(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})^{2}r^{\prime}-\frac{4(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2}){r^{\prime}}^{2}}{(1-s^{\prime})}

(9.1)

\displaystyle-\frac{{r^{\prime}}^{3}}{(1-s^{\prime})^{2}}\Big\}\mu(LDW)-(1-s^{\prime})(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})\Big\{(1-s^{\prime})^{2}-{r^{\prime}}^{2}+(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2}){r^{\prime}}^{2}\Big\}\mu(LDL),

and the corresponding weight function inequality is given by

(9.2)

\displaystyle w(\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}\mu)\leqslant{}

\displaystyle w(\mu)-\frac{(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})\{(1-s^{\prime})(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})-r^{\prime}\}^{2}}{1-s^{\prime}}\mu(DD).

We now deduce, from (
9.2
), the conclusion we desire, i.e.
\mu(D)=0
for any translation-invariant and reflection-invariant probability distribution
\mu
that is stationary for
\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
. As seen in §
3.3
,
\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
is equivalent to
\G_{p,q,r}
if we set
q=0
and let
p
and
r
equal suitable functions of
r^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
, as given by (
3.14
) and (
3.15
). From (
3.15
), we see that
r^{\prime}=0\implies r=0
, so that we are back to the set-up of
[
24
]
, with
q=0
and
p=2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2}
, when
r^{\prime}=0
. Our desired conclusion , therefore, follows from
[
24
]
when
r^{\prime}=0
. It thus suffices for us to consider
r^{\prime}>0
.

Since
r^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
are sufficiently small, we have
(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})>0
, and because of (
2.6
), we have
(1-s^{\prime})(2s^{\prime}-{s^{\prime}}^{2})-r^{\prime}>0
as well, so that the coefficient of
\mu(DD)
in (
9.2
) is strictly positive. Consequently, when
\mu
is stationary for
\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
, the parameters
r^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
are sufficiently small and they satisfy the inequality in (
2.6
), we have
\mu(DD)=0
, which, in turn, implies that
\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}\mu(DD)=0
. By an application of Lemma
8.2
with
\G_{p,q,r}
replaced by
\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
(where we set
\mathcal{C}=(D,D)_{0,1}
and consider
\mathcal{D}\in\left\{(W,D,W)_{0,1,2},(W,D,L)_{0,1,2},(L,D,L)_{0,1,2}\right\}
) and using (
3.8
) through (
3.13
), we see that

(1-s^{\prime})^{2}(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})^{2}\mu(WDW)+2r^{\prime}(1-s^{\prime})(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})^{2}\mu(WDL)+{r^{\prime}}^{2}(1-r^{\prime}-s^{\prime})^{2}\mu(LDL)\leqslant\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}\mu(DD),

so that, for
r^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
sufficiently small,
r^{\prime}>0
, and
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})
satisfying (
2.6
), we have
\mu(WDW)=\mu(WDL)=\mu(LDW)=\mu(LDL)=0
when
\mu
is any translation-invariant and reflection-invariant stationary distribution for
\E_{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}}
. Moreover, we already know that
\mu(WDD)=\mu(DDW)=\mu(LDD)=\mu(DDL)=\mu(DDD)=0
as each of these is bounded above by
\mu(DD)
. Combining all of these, we obtain
\mu(D)=0
, as desired.

When
(r^{\prime},s^{\prime})
belongs to the regime given by
(B2)
, the weight function takes various forms depending on the subset of
(0.157175,1]
that the parameter
r^{\prime}
belongs to. When
s^{\prime}=0
and
r^{\prime}\in[0.4564,1]
, it suffices to consider the weight function

(9.3)

w(\mu)=\mu(D)+\mu(WD)+\mu(LWD)-2r^{\prime}\mu(WD),

and the corresponding weight function inequality is given by

\displaystyle w(\E_{r^{\prime},0}\mu)\leqslant{}

\displaystyle w(\mu)-{r^{\prime}}^{2}\mu(DD)+(2r^{\prime}-6{r^{\prime}}^{2}+4{r^{\prime}}^{3}-{r^{\prime}}^{4})\mu(LD)-(3r^{\prime}-3{r^{\prime}}^{2}+{r^{\prime}}^{3})\mu(LWD)

\displaystyle-(1-r^{\prime})^{2}(1-r^{\prime}+2{r^{\prime}}^{2})\mu(LDL)-2{r^{\prime}}^{2}(1-r^{\prime})^{2}\mu(LDD)-r^{\prime}(1-r^{\prime})^{2}(1+r^{\prime})\mu(LDW)

(9.4)

\displaystyle-r^{\prime}(1-r^{\prime})^{4}\mu(LLD)-r^{\prime}(1-r^{\prime})^{3}\mu(DLD).

When
s^{\prime}=0
and
r^{\prime}\in(0.201383,0.4564)
, the weight function that yields the desired conclusion is

(9.5)

w(\mu)=\mu(D)+\mu(WD)+\mu(LWD)-2r^{\prime}\mu(WD)-(3r^{\prime}-3{r^{\prime}}^{2}+{r^{\prime}}^{3})\mu(LWD),

and the corresponding weight function inequality is given by

\displaystyle w(\E_{r^{\prime},0}\mu)\leqslant{}

\displaystyle w(\mu)-{r^{\prime}}^{2}\mu(DD)+(2r^{\prime}-14{r^{\prime}}^{2}+23{r^{\prime}}^{3}-14{r^{\prime}}^{4}+3{r^{\prime}}^{6}-{r^{\prime}}^{7})\mu(LD)

\displaystyle-(1-r^{\prime})^{2}(1-r^{\prime}-3{r^{\prime}}^{2}+3{r^{\prime}}^{3}-{r^{\prime}}^{4})\mu(LDL)-r^{\prime}(1-r^{\prime})^{2}(1-r^{\prime}-3{r^{\prime}}^{2}+3{r^{\prime}}^{3}-{r^{\prime}}^{4})\mu(LDW)

\displaystyle-r^{\prime}(1-r^{\prime})^{2}({r^{\prime}}^{5}-2{r^{\prime}}^{4}-{r^{\prime}}^{3}+7{r^{\prime}}^{2}-5r^{\prime}+1)\mu(LLD)-r^{\prime}(1-r^{\prime})^{6}\mu(DLD)

(9.6)

\displaystyle-(3r^{\prime}-3{r^{\prime}}^{2}+{r^{\prime}}^{3})r^{\prime}(1-r^{\prime})^{2}\mu(LWD).

For the final sub-regime, i.e. where
s^{\prime}=0
and

\displaystyle w(\mu)={}

\displaystyle\mu(D)+\mu(WD)+\mu(LWD)-2r^{\prime}\mu(WD)-(3r^{\prime}-3{r^{\prime}}^{2}+{r^{\prime}}^{3})\mu(LWD)-r^{\prime}(1-r^{\prime})^{6}\mu(LLWD)

and the corresponding weight function inequality is of the form

\displaystyle w(\E_{r^{\prime},0}\mu)\leqslant{}

\displaystyle+{r^{\prime}}^{2}(1-r^{\prime})(1+r^{\prime})\mu(DDD)+{r^{\prime}}^{3}(1-r^{\prime})\mu(DDL)+{r^{\prime}}^{2}(1-r^{\prime})\mu(LDW)+{r^{\prime}}^{2}(1-r^{\prime})(1+r^{\prime})\mu(LDD)

\displaystyle w_{1}(\mu)={}

\displaystyle w_{0}(\mu)-(1-p)(1-r)\{2p-4r+p^{2}+3r^{2}+2pr-2p^{2}r-6pr^{2}\}\mu(LD)-(1-p)(1-r)

\displaystyle\{3r+2p^{2}+2r^{2}-pr-2p^{2}r-4pr^{2}-r^{3}\}\mu(LDW)-(1-p)^{2}(1-r)(1-r^{2}+pr^{2})\mu(LDL)

(9.21)

\displaystyle-(1-p)(1-r)\{2p+r+p^{2}+2r^{2}-p^{2}r-5pr^{2}\}\mu(LWD),

which is exactly the same as what we stated in (
9.1
) (once we replace
w_{1}
by
w
and set
p
and
r
to be the functions of
r^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}
as given by (
9.12
)). In order to understand how the update in (
9.21
) impacts the weight function inequality in (

), we need to compute
\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LD)
,
\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LDW)
,
\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LDL)
and
\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LDW)
, but only partially, just as we did in (

), (
8.67
), (
8.68
) and (
8.69
):

\displaystyle\bullet(1-p)^{2}(1-r)\mu(WWD)\leqslant\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LD)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

\displaystyle-(1-p)(1-r)\{2p-4r+p^{2}+3r^{2}+2pr-2p^{2}r-6pr^{2}\}\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LD)\leqslant-(1-p)(1-r)

\displaystyle\{2p-4r+p^{2}+3r^{2}+2pr-2p^{2}r-6pr^{2}\}(1-p)^{2}(1-r)\mu(WWD)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

\displaystyle-(1-p)(1-r)\{2p-4r+p^{2}+3r^{2}+2pr-2p^{2}r-6pr^{2}\}\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LD)\leqslant-(1-p)^{3}(1-r)^{2}

(9.22)

\displaystyle\{2p-4r+p^{2}+3r^{2}+2pr-2p^{2}r-6pr^{2}\}\mu(WWD)

(the second step of the derivation of (
9.22
) is true because the coefficient
-(1-p)(1-r)\{2p-4r+p^{2}+3r^{2}+2pr-2p^{2}r-6pr^{2}\}
is non-positive when (
9.13
) holds and
p
and
r
are both sufficiently small);

\displaystyle\bullet p(1-p)^{2}(1-r)\{\mu(WWDW)+\mu(WWDD)\}\leqslant\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LDW)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

\displaystyle-(1-p)(1-r)\{3r+2p^{2}+2r^{2}-pr-2p^{2}r-4pr^{2}-r^{3}\}\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LDW)\leqslant-(1-p)(1-r)

\displaystyle\{3r+2p^{2}+2r^{2}-pr-2p^{2}r-4pr^{2}-r^{3}\}p(1-p)^{2}(1-r)\{\mu(WWDW)+\mu(WWDD)\}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

\displaystyle-(1-p)(1-r)\{3r+2p^{2}+2r^{2}-pr-2p^{2}r-4pr^{2}-r^{3}\}\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LDW)\leqslant-p(1-p)^{3}(1-r)^{2}

(9.23)

\displaystyle\{3r+2p^{2}+2r^{2}-pr-2p^{2}r-4pr^{2}-r^{3}\}\{\mu(WWDW)+\mu(WWDD)\},

\displaystyle\bullet r(1-p)^{3}(1-r)\mu(WWDW)+r^{2}(1-p)^{3}(1-r)\mu(WWDD)\leqslant\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LDL)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

\displaystyle-(1-p)^{2}(1-r)(1-r^{2}+pr^{2})\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LDL)\leqslant-(1-p)^{2}(1-r)(1-r^{2}+pr^{2})\{r(1-p)^{3}(1-r)

\displaystyle\mu(WWDW)+r^{2}(1-p)^{3}(1-r)\mu(WWDD)\}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

\displaystyle-(1-p)^{2}(1-r)(1-r^{2}+pr^{2})\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LDL)\leqslant-r(1-p)^{5}(1-r)^{2}(1-r^{2}+pr^{2})\mu(WWDW)

(9.24)

\displaystyle-r^{2}(1-p)^{5}(1-r)^{2}(1-r^{2}+pr^{2})\mu(WWDD);

and finally,

\displaystyle\bullet p(1-p)^{2}(1-r)\{\mu(WWDW)+\mu(WWWD)\}+p(1-p)^{2}(1-r)(1+r)\mu(WWDD)\leqslant\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LWD)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

\displaystyle-(1-p)(1-r)\{2p+r+p^{2}+2r^{2}-p^{2}r-5pr^{2}\}\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LWD)\leqslant-(1-p)(1-r)\{2p+r+p^{2}+2r^{2}

\displaystyle-p^{2}r-5pr^{2}\}[p(1-p)^{2}(1-r)\{\mu(WWDW)+\mu(WWWD)\}+p(1-p)^{2}(1-r)(1+r)\mu(WWDD)]

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow{}

\displaystyle-(1-p)(1-r)\{2p+r+p^{2}+2r^{2}-p^{2}r-5pr^{2}\}\G_{p,0,r}\mu(LWD)\leqslant-p(1-p)^{3}(1-r)^{2}\{2p+r+p^{2}

\displaystyle+2r^{2}-p^{2}r-5pr^{2}\}\{\mu(WWDW)+\mu(WWWD)\}-p(1-p)^{3}(1-r)^{2}(1+r)\{2p+r+p^{2}+2r^{2}

(9.25)

\displaystyle-p^{2}r-5pr^{2}\}\mu(WWDD).

Implementing the same idea as that used to derive (

), the update in (

) transforms the weight function inequality in (

) as follows, using the bounds obtained in (
